# Generation 4: verification of Last Copper's kill gates and a post-cutoff fact sweep (2026-10-21)

Eighteen research agents (5 parallel workflows) answered the Gen 3 open questions and swept dated facts from after mid-2026. The shared web-search budget ran out partway through, so several agents worked from direct fetches of primary sources (FCC, eCFR, SEC, Verizon, Rutgers). Each claim keeps its status: verified, contradicted or unverified.

## 0. Founder-verified primary sources (read directly, text saved in `research/sources/`)

Verizon "may require customers ... to migrate services off of copper facilities well before" that date. New Brunswick is not listed. | The original hook. |

| eCFR **47 CFR 63.602** version history | Section removed and reserved as of 2026-10-15. | eCFR **47 CFR 63.71**, 2026-10-01 vs 2026-10-15 | Technology-transition discontinuances are now auto-granted if the carrier certifies that every location has at least one of: facilities-based VoIP, mobile wireless at 5/1 Mbps, high-cost-program voice, or its own tested voice service. Gone: the old (a)(6) customer notices that the replacement "may not provide line power", the customer's "security responsibilities" and "a list of the steps the customer may take to ensure safe use". Added: 911-authority coordination. | The burden of finding and proving lifelines moves to building owners, who mostly do not know which lines are lifelines. |
| eCFR **47 CFR 51.333**, 2026-10-01 vs 2026-10-15 | The copper-retirement objection and resolution procedure is removed (the old (f) said a LEC "may not retire those copper facilities" until objections were resolved). Discovered Jan 12, 2026 during First Office Application testing. The FCC granted a conditional waiver. | A documented silent failure during a Verizon transition, caught by testing. |
| eCFR **47 CFR 63.602**, Jan 2026 text | The three adequate-replacement criteria quoted in full (service quality; 911, security and accessibility; interoperability). | Shows exactly what was removed. |

**Corrections to Gen 3 claims:**
1. "Busch, Livingston and SHI are served by Piscataway": unverified, and partly contradicted. New Brunswick (NBWKNJNB) also serves Piscataway addresses, and Rutgers' main numbers sit on a CLEC.
2. "NJ IBC 3001.2 mandatory mid-Feb 2027": wrong. It is not retroactive; Feb 17, 2027 is only the permit-application cutover.
3. "Deaf text mode is novel": wrong. It is code-required on new cars and already shipped by OEMs.
4. "Nobody tests speaker and mic": wrong for IP emergency phones (Talkaphone, Code Blue, Aiphone). It is true for analog stations moved onto cellular gateways.
5. "Past winners were all consumer apps": wrong (AeroBin).
6. "BPC100 with 24-hour battery for elevators": wrong. It runs on AA cells with 23 h standby and 3 h talk, supports Contact-ID only, and is not marketed for elevators. Verizon's life-safety channel is CSG POTSolve or MarketSpark.

**Operational note:** one research agent reported that a single SEC EDGAR request carried the user's email in its User-Agent header. It was not repeated. All later agents were instructed never to send personal data in headers, URLs or queries.


## 1. How Rutgers gets phone service, and which life-safety lines could be on Verizon copper (`rutgers_telecom`, confidence medium)

Rutgers runs its own voice system: Cisco desk phones with Cisco Unity voicemail and Webex, plus Microsoft Teams Phone (page created Feb 2026). OIT provides 'Analog, Emergency, VoIP, and Call Centers' voice services over campus copper and conduit that Rutgers owns. The VoIP phone numbers (848-445, 848-932) belong to PAETEC ITEL (Windstream), not Verizon. The older 732-445, 732-932 and 732-743 blocks are Verizon NJ numbers assigned to the Piscataway 5ESS at 4 Skiles Ave, the central office whose copper retires on or after Dec 10 2026. Number porting may mean Verizon no longer serves them. Two of the three main life-safety line types have already left phone lines (verified). In 2020, NAPCO StarLink IP-plus-cellular fire communicators went into 450 buildings, replacing 'the traditional telephone lines'. In 2023, blue lights were replaced with solar units that send daily health reports and are monitored by Knightscope. Elevator phones are the gap: Rutgers standards name Avire, Wurtec and Kings III but not the line type. There is no sourced count. My judgment: only a small minority of life-safety lines plausibly ride Verizon NJ copper, mostly elevator phones on direct carrier lines, off-campus or leased sites, and any remaining Centrex or business lines. Unverified: the 2020 Cisco cutover date, the state voice contract (its page needs a Rutgers login), the StarLink and Knightscope carriers, and procurement bids (the portal is rendered in JavaScript). The web-search budget ran out after 14 searches.

**Implication for Last Copper.** This weakens Last Copper considerably and probably fails Gate A for the Rutgers countdown story. The subset the briefing expected to be the main Verizon-copper exposure, fire-alarm communicators, was moved to NAPCO StarLink IP-plus-cellular units in 450 buildings in 2020. Blue-light phones were replaced in 2023 with Knightscope-monitored solar units that already send daily health reports. That pre-empts the roadmap item and lets a judge say automated daily supervision already exists here. OIT runs its own analog and emergency voice service over Rutgers-owned copper and conduit, and its VoIP phone numbers come from a competing carrier, not Verizon. On the main campuses, life-safety lines therefore most likely hang off Rutgers' own system rather than Verizon loops that Verizon will cut. What survives: (1) elevator phones, whose line type is undocumented and which are the only plausible large remaining exposure; (2) the striking fact that Rutgers' own 732-445, 732-932 and 732-743 blocks are assigned to the retiring Piscataway central office, though porting may make this moot; (3) PROVE as a vendor-agnostic nightly acoustic test for elevator phones, which needs no copper deadline. Before Oct 31, ask OIT three questions: are elevator phones analog ports on the Cisco system, Kings III or other carrier lines, or Verizon business lines? Which carrier serves 732-445, 732-932 and 732-743 today? Which carrier version are the StarLink units? If the answers are 'OIT analog ports' and 'ported', drop the Dec 10 countdown framing.

**Idea seeds:**
- Correlated-failure risk: 450 Rutgers fire-alarm communicators are NAPCO StarLink IP+cellular units. If most ride one carrier and the IP path shares campus network failure modes, one carrier or core outage could silence fire signaling campus-wide (carrier unverified).
- Teams Phone migration (2026) turns each migrated Cisco desk phone into a 911-only device and moves calling to Teams clients. That creates a fleet of 911-only phones plus the dispatchable-location (RAY BAUM'S) problem for Teams users. Verizon could pitch Operator Connect for Teams (verify Verizon's offering).
- Rutgers' VoIP numbers (848-445, 848-932) belong to PAETEC/Windstream, not Verizon. A Verizon win-back of campus PSTN and SIP trunks around the Teams Phone rollout is a concrete E&PS revenue story.
- Knightscope blue lights already produce daily system-health reports. They are a primary data source a student team could request, for example to analyze uptime and fault patterns with OIT and Public Safety.
- Rutgers owns its own aging copper plant from RUNet 2000 (about 25 years old, 64 miles of conduit). 'Rutgers' own copper sunset' is an internal-migration idea that does not depend on Verizon's notice.
- Small fleets of non-voice telemetry still on telephone lines (Interlink RTUs at sewage pump stations, Silent Knight DACT inputs) are LTE-M candidates.
- Rutgers' legacy 732-445, 732-932 and 732-743 numbers, which include the RUPD non-emergency line, are assigned to Verizon's Piscataway 5ESS. If any are still served there as Centrex or business lines, they are exposed to TDM and copper discontinuance. Ask OIT.

## 2. Which Verizon wire center serves Busch, Livingston, SHI and RBHS (`wire_center`, confidence medium)

Verified from LERG-derived numbering data (localcallingguide, updated 2026-10-10): Rutgers' legacy exchanges 732-445 and 732-932 and the RBHS exchange 732-235 belong to the New Brunswick rate center. Verizon NJ (OCN 9206, the ILEC) holds them on the Piscataway 5ESS host PSWYNJPIDS5 (4 Skiles Ave). Rutgers' current main blocks 848-445 and 848-932 are not Verizon numbers: they belong to PAETEC ITEL, LLC (OCN 829J, a CLEC) on switch WORNNJWOX1X. The block dates read 2010-10-17. That puts Rutgers' main phone numbers on a competing carrier, not on Verizon. The NXX evidence cannot settle the wire center. PSWYNJPIDS5 hosts a remote switch, NBWKNJNBRS2, located in the New Brunswick CO at 18 Paterson St, and that wire center is not retiring. Verizon's own 2016 notice 2016-03-A-NJ lists the New Brunswick wire center (NBWKNJNB) serving Piscataway Township addresses: Ross Hall Blvd N/S and 1355-1401 River Rd, which lie between SHI Stadium and Livingston. The Edison 5ESS also serves New Brunswick rate-center exchanges. Best estimate, unverified: RBHS on Hoes Lane is very likely PSWYNJPI (0.4-1 km from the CO). Busch is probably PSWYNJPI (2.0 km vs 3.3 km to New Brunswick). SHI Stadium and Livingston are uncertain and could be PSWYNJPI, NBWKNJNB or EDSNNJED. Only Verizon customer service records (CSRs) or bills can settle it: the serving office or wire center and circuit IDs for each line. Limits: only 3 WebSearch calls ran before the shared search budget ran out. I replaced searches with direct fetches of primary sources. Port status of individual numbers is not public.

**Implication for Last Copper.** This weakens the champion but does not kill it. The Gen 3 line "Verizon is retiring its Piscataway copper (Busch, Livingston and SHI Stadium are in Piscataway)" is not supported and is partly contradicted. Verizon's own 2016 exhibit shows the New Brunswick wire center, which is not retiring, serving Piscataway addresses (Ross Hall Blvd and River Rd 1355-1401) that sit right between SHI and Livingston. The Edison wire center, also not retiring, serves New Brunswick rate-center numbers too. Only RBHS on Hoes Lane (under 1 km from 4 Skiles Ave) is very likely PSWYNJPI, and Busch is probable. SHI and Livingston could be served by any of three wire centers. Rutgers' main voice numbers (848-445/932) have been on a CLEC (PAETEC ITEL) since about 2010, and campus phones are Cisco/Teams. Any Verizon exposure is therefore limited to standalone POTS lines (elevator phones, fire-alarm dialers, fax). Some may sit on CLEC service riding Verizon copper loops; the notice says interconnecting carriers must make alternative arrangements. The video must not show the Piscataway row and claim it covers Busch, Livingston and the stadium. Safe wording: "Verizon is retiring the copper in the Piscataway wire center, which serves Rutgers' Piscataway health-sciences campus and possibly parts of Busch." Gate A now hinges on Verizon CSRs and bills: the LSO or serving wire center per line, and the CLLI in circuit IDs, for Rutgers service addresses in 08854. A telephone number's NXX cannot answer it, because the Piscataway host switch also runs a remote in the New Brunswick CO and numbers can be ported. If Gate A cannot be verified by Oct 31, lead with the national program (62 NJ wire centers, about 1,566 nationally) and use 'which wire center serves my building?' as the problem framing.

**Idea seeds:**
- 'Am I on the list?' resolver. Verizon's 2026 notices name only wire centers ('all remaining locations'). Building owners think in townships, but Piscataway Township is split across at least two rate centers (New Brunswick and Dunellen) and at least two wire centers (PSWYNJPI and NBWKNJNB; Dunellen likely too). An address-to-wire-center resolver could be built from Verizon's own address-level exhibits (2016-03-A-NJ, 2019-01-B-NJ) plus switch and rate-center data, so owners know whether their life-safety lines are affected.
- Hidden CLEC exposure. The New Brunswick rate center has dozens of CLEC and VoIP blocks (PAETEC ITEL, Comcast IP Phone, Lightpath, Level 3, Bandwidth, Onvoy, YMAX, Bluebird on PSWYNJBXDS0). Customers of a CLEC that leases Verizon copper loops are hit by the retirement without ever receiving Verizon's notice ('interconnecting carriers will need to make alternative arrangements'). A wholesale or CLEC-customer warning angle exists.
- Remote-switch dependency. The 5ESS remote in the New Brunswick CO (NBWKNJNBRS2) is homed on the Piscataway host (PSWYNJPIDS5), and the 2026 notice explicitly targets TDM/SS7-dependent services. A future Piscataway host-switch retirement could reach New Brunswick lines that the copper notice never names. This is speculative and needs verification.
- Numbers are not locations. The Rutgers Cancer Institute in New Brunswick publishes 732-235 numbers homed on the Piscataway switch. Any FIND-style classifier or outreach list that maps lines by NXX will misassign them; it must use the service address and serving wire center from Verizon's records.
- Rutgers still advertises legacy Verizon-exchange numbers (RUPD 732-932-7211, EOHSI fax 732-445-0131). An OPRA request or the OIT bill would show whether these lines are still Verizon TDM lines. That is concrete primary data only Rutgers students can obtain.

## 3. Prior art: automated testing of elevator emergency phones (`elevator_prior_art`, confidence medium)

No web searches ran: the shared search budget was exhausted (0 of 8 requested), so everything below comes from about 60 directly fetched primary pages and PDFs. VERIFIED: A17.1 has required line verification since the 2007/b-2009 era (2.27.1.1.6), with a failure signal within 24 h (Interp. 13-343). By rule it is a line check that 'shall not require activation of the two-way communication link(s)', so it never tests the car's mic or speaker. A17.1-2019/2022 added in-car text, YES/NO answers and video (2.27.1.1.3(c)-(e),(k)), location on demand (f) and a 45 s redirect. ASME said in 2013 that automated testing through routing systems is allowed. Incumbents: RATH line checks every 10 min to 23 h; the JANUS 'end-to-end' claim is only line presence; Viking runs a daily dial-tone test, on-demand IP mic and speaker diagnostics, and auto-answer silent monitoring. Location messages (Viking, Talkaphone, RATH) and Deaf text/video (RATH SmartView 2 at $9.99/device/mo, Wurtec Wur-Com, Otis ONE Pro, MAD, Schindler) are already shipping. POTS gateways (AirDial, MarketSpark, EPIK, POTSolve) supervise power, battery and connectivity but not audio. Automated audio self-tests do exist: 2N LiftIP tests audio after its 3-day check calls, and SafeLine raises mic/speaker failure alarms; EN 81-28 requires a test call every 3 days or less. AVIRE SENTINEL already sells managed lift-line migration with automated test calls for the UK copper switch-off in Jan 2027. UNVERIFIED: Kings III testing (site blocked), Telguard, KONE/TKE, how 2N's test works, and patents.

**Implication for Last Copper.** This weakens the champion and narrows it a lot, but does not kill it. Claims that are now false or indefensible: 'nobody tests these lines', 'nightly testing is new', 'the text mode for Deaf riders is ours', 'the location announcement is ours', and 'migrating and supervising lifelines through a copper shutdown is new'. UK SENTINEL does the last one, and MarketSpark already pitches it to campuses. Automated in-car audio self-test also exists (2N LiftIP, SafeLine), so a generic 'speaker/mic self-test' is not novel. Defensible white space, unchanged by everything found: (1) an end-to-end, two-direction in-band audio test that goes through the actual migrated carrier voice path to the actual answering console, for legacy analog car phones that have no self-test. The A17.1 line check is barred from making that call, and the gateways only supervise power and connectivity. (2) Proof that the location the answering point actually receives matches the car, since RATH labels are installer recordings and Ooma makes the customer 'solely responsible'. (3) Measured answer and 45 s redirect times. (4) A carrier-side, wire-center-level cutover health signal for Verizon. No vendor found offers a carrier-facing view; every vendor dashboard is per customer. Pitch fix: lead with 'the code checks the wire, not the voice' (2.27.1.1.6(a) plus Interp. 13-343's 24 h) and present SENTINEL and 2N as proof the market exists. Expect a Verizon or Facilities judge to name Kings III, RATH or MarketSpark; have the comparison slide ready.

| verified | ASME A17.1-2019 added in-car two-way text and video, and both remain in A17.1-2022. 2.27.1.1.3(c), (d) and (e) cover an acknowledgement message, messages that get answers from passengers who cannot speak or hear, and a 'help is on the way' message. (k) requires video of the whole car floor. - A17.1 2.27.1.1.6(a) bars activating the two-way link for line verification. That gives a citable reason why US code-compliant monitoring cannot detect a dead car mic or speaker.
- RATH SmartView 2 needs a modem-capable analog path. A Verizon gateway compatibility lab (BPC100 and ATEL vs SmartView/Wur-Com text and video) could be a concrete Verizon-specific deliverable.

- Primary-data hook: audit Rutgers cars for installer-recorded location messages that are wrong or stale (RATH/Viking/Talkaphone) and measure the answer and redirect times against the 45 s rule.
- EN 81-28's 3-day test call vs the US 24 h line check: a 'harmonize up' advocacy angle for ASME A17 or NJ DCA comments.
- Viking auto-answer 'Silent Monitoring' is an existing feature. The zero-install dial-in check has a precedent, but so does its privacy risk.
- MarketSpark's Oct 2026 post attacks 'carrier-provided adapters' (Verizon BPC100). Verizon has a reason to want a proof layer that defends its own path.

## 4. Who answers a Rutgers elevator HELP call (`who_answers`, confidence medium)

No public Rutgers record says who answers a campus elevator HELP call; this stays UNVERIFIED and needs a direct OIT/Facilities/RUPD question. Verified context: RUSSECC is Rutgers' 24/7 PSAP and the 'first point of contact for all 911 calls and non-emergency calls made on Rutgers campuses'. Facilities' call center works only 7:00-4:30. Rutgers' Aug 2026 elevator standard names Avire, Wurtec and Kings III phones and says to coordinate video monitoring with 'Rutgers Emergency Operations'. Approved installers are KONE, Otis and TK Elevator, and new cars get 24-hour callback with a 1-hour emergency response. The current maintenance contractor is unverified (procurement needs a login). Peer model (verified): at UNC-Chapel Hill, cellular elevator phones go to a third-party monitor paid through the elevator maintenance contract, and 'UNC Police are not monitoring'. The three US models are police/PSAP, the OEM call center (OTISLINE) and a monitoring firm (Kings III's own dispatch center). NJ keeps no entrapment statistic (only injury or damage accidents are reportable), and inspections are at most yearly. City data (verified): since 2023, Chicago inspectors cited an emergency-phone item in 1,613 of 6,725 inspections that drew an elevator maintain/repair violation (24%), including wrong address, wrong car ID and calls to the old contractor. FDNY logged 27,247 occupied elevator emergencies in 2025. No Rutgers entrapment news or Reddit item was found: the web-search budget was exhausted (0 searches ran), so only direct fetches and open-data APIs were used.

**Implication for Last Copper.** Mixed: the PROVE half gets stronger, the copper-countdown half gets weaker. STRONGER: live inspection data shows the failures PROVE targets are real and recurring after installation, and line monitoring does not catch them. Chicago inspectors cite phones that announce the wrong city ('Nashville, TN'), give the wrong car ID, dial the previous maintenance contractor, or dial 911 instead of a live answering service, and some phones are re-cited years later. That is direct evidence for location assurance and answer-routing checks. Contractor and line changes create silent misrouting, which mirrors a copper cutover. NJ inspects at most yearly and does not record entrapments, so nobody measures this in NJ. FDNY's 27k occupied entrapments a year supports Ubiquity. WEAKER: Rutgers names Kings III as an approved phone vendor, and Kings III sells cellular phones with its own dispatch center. The UNC precedent, where a third-party monitor is paid through the elevator maintenance contract and police do not monitor, is a common model. Many Rutgers cars may therefore already be off Verizon copper and answered by a vendor, which makes the Dec 10 countdown moot for them, and the buyer would be Facilities' elevator contract rather than OIT. NC also warns that changing the phone service can trigger a full A17.1-2019 video and text upgrade (about $0.8M per car if it escalates to modernization), so MOVE carries a hidden cost and AHJ risk. Rutgers being its own construction AHJ under the DCA MOU helps pilot feasibility. Bottom line: lead with PROVE as 'who actually picks up, and do they know where you are'. Use the Chicago and NYC data now and the Rutgers HELP audit as the cold open. Demote copper to a 'transition moment' rather than the premise. The single most important unknown is still the answer to 'who answers at Rutgers', which needs a direct OIT/Facilities/RUPD question this week.

## 5. Prior art and rules: blue-light phones and fire-alarm communicators (`bluelight_firealarm_prior_art`, confidence medium)

Incumbents already self-test speakers and mics on IP call stations. Talkaphone VOIP-500 (2013) plays a tone through its speaker and records it on its mic. Code Blue IP5000 (2011) reports speaker and mic failures. Aiphone IX Device Check beeps to test speaker and mic daily or every 10-60 minutes, and Commend does the same. The gap is analog stations moved onto cellular. Talkaphone's VoLTE retrofit kit (AT&T SIM) supervises only gateway online/offline and SIM status. Its notes say DTMF-based Talk-A-Lert polling 'may not function as intended' over cellular, and the gateway cannot dial 911. NFPA 72 (2013/2019 text, quoted by AHJs) allows a single path unless the AHJ prohibits it, supervised every 60 minutes or less. Multiple paths are supervised every 6 hours or less. A DACT needs one phone line plus a second means, which can be cellular. I could not read the 2022 text. NJ adopts IBC/2024; that this brings in NFPA 72-2022 is my inference. Campuses: UNC-CH fire alarms were all on POTS in 2018 and it chose mesh radio. NAPCO said in 2020 that Rutgers was installing StarLink dual-path communicators in 450 buildings. Yet Rutgers' March 2025 design standard still specifies a DACT with two phone lines for New Brunswick. Rutgers Emergency Services is the fire AHJ. Failures: UAA's blue lights were dead about 3 years from copper faults. FCC 26-19 (Mar 2026) refused alarm-codec protections. I found no dated report of a fire-alarm monitoring failure. Search budget ran out after 16 searches.

**Implication for Last Copper.** Mixed. This narrows the champion rather than killing it. (1) Its novelty claim gets weaker. Speaker/mic self-tests have been standard on IP emergency phones for over a decade: Talkaphone 2013, Code Blue 2011 and UL 2017 fault reporting on all its phones, Aiphone IX scheduled Device Check, Commend. A judge from security or facilities will know this. Do not say 'nobody tests the speaker and mic'. (2) The real, sourced gap is analog stations moved onto cellular gateways, which is exactly the population Last Copper moves. Talkaphone's own retrofit-kit page says DTMF-based Talk-A-Lert polling and diagnostics 'may not function as intended' over cellular, that the gateway cannot dial 911, and that cloud monitoring covers only online/offline and SIM status. That makes it the perfect slide: 'Moving to cellular removes the self-test; PROVE puts it back, on Verizon.' The incumbent kit also ships with AT&T SIMs only, which is a Verizon competitive opening. (3) Fire alarms should leave the core scope. Rutgers apparently began moving fire-alarm communicators to NAPCO StarLink dual-path cellular/IP in 2020 (450 buildings). Communicators are listed equipment under NFPA 72/UL 864 and Rutgers Emergency Services is the AHJ, and the champion already says 'never build fire-alarm communicators'. The March 2025 Rutgers design standard still specifies two phone lines into a Silent Knight DACT for New Brunswick. Gate A should therefore ask which buildings still have DACT lines on Verizon copper and which carrier StarLink uses. If it is Verizon, those communicators feed Cutover Watch. If AT&T, it is a retention story. (4) Blue lights strengthen the roadmap. No code mandate exists (NC SCO), copper failures go unnoticed for years (UAA), and campus mesh-radio fire networks cannot carry two-way voice (UNC-CH). That leaves blue lights, refuge and elevator voice as the endpoints without a standard supervision story, which is PROVE's lane. (5) Regulatory hook: FCC 26-19 (Mar 2026) refused alarm-codec protections and only 'encourages' carriers to prevent alarm signaling failures. That gives Verizon a reason to want carrier-side proof.

**Idea seeds:**
- 'The self-test you lose when you leave copper': Talkaphone documents that its DTMF polling of analog blue-light stations may fail over cellular and that its kit cannot dial 911. A Verizon-native (ThingSpace LTE-M) supervision layer would restore end-to-end proof for migrated analog lifelines. This sharpens PROVE.
- FCC 26-19 para 31 (released 2026-03-27) refused alarm-codec protections and only 'encourages carriers to ensure their IP networks are appropriately configured so as to prevent alarm signaling failures'. A Verizon 'Alarm-Safe Line' could place automated Contact ID/SIA test calls through each replacement voice path (G.711, RFC 2833 handling) to receiver numbers. It would be a carrier-side answer to AICC's 5 million dialer panels, using a post-mid-2026 fact.
- The incumbent blue-light retrofit kit (Talkaphone VoLTE, Feb 2025) bundles AT&T SIMs only. A Verizon-certified blue-light/refuge retrofit with nightly proof would be a channel/partner play for Verizon E&PS.
- Rutgers primary data only students can gather: the 2020 NAPCO StarLink rollout (450 buildings) vs the March 2025 design standard that still requires two phone lines into a Silent Knight 5104 DACT. Audit which buildings still have DACT lines on Verizon copper, which carrier StarLink rides, and whether the standard is stale.
- Answering-point bottleneck: NC's State Capitol Police receiving station could not accept cellular alarm calls (2022). Check whether RUSSECC/RUPD's receivers accept cellular or IP formats, and who answers blue-light calls after migration.
- UNC-CH campus fire-alarm network cost benchmark: mesh radio $3.37M vs Ethernet IP $31.23M. That gives a campus-scale comparator for a Verizon LTE-M fire/alarm path offering, and shows mesh radio cannot carry voice endpoints.
- Video hook: UAA blue light phones failed for about 3 years because of copper and were found only by annual night safety walks. Silent failure of unsupervised emergency phones is documented.
- Aiphone IX supervision is limited: call-button checks 'do not detect damage', and IP phones 'cannot perform monitoring'. A cross-vendor supervision layer could cover what single-vendor tools miss.

## 6. New Jersey codes: IBC 3001.2, A17.1 edition, retroactivity (`nj_codes`, confidence high)

VERIFIED: NJ DCA adopted R.2026 d.086 (filed July 15, 2026; effective Aug 17, 2026). It makes the 2024 IBC the building subcode and drops NJ's old deletion of IBC 3001.2. The 2024 3001.2 requires a two-way system with 'both visual and audible communication modes' that complies with ASME A17.1 in each car. The six-month grace period (5:23-1.6) is a choice for permit applicants: until about Feb 17, 2027 they may file under the old code. It is not a retrofit deadline. VERIFIED from code text, conclusion inferred: 3001.2 does NOT reach existing elevators. Existing devices are maintained to the standard 'in effect at the time of the installation and/or alteration' (5:23-12.2(b)), and no retroactive item in 5:23-12.12 covers communications. 3001.2 reaches new or completely replaced elevators (5:23-6.9(d)) and possibly signaling-device alterations (A17.1 8.7.2.28, wording unverified). Renovations over 50% trigger A17.3-2000 car-signaling items instead. Editions: new work uses the 'most recent edition of ASME A17.1 referenced in the building subcode', which is A17.1-2022 under IBC/2024 (2019 before). Existing-elevator retrofits cite A17.3-1993 and A17.3-2000. Lines and monitoring: no NJ-specific rule found. A17.1 2.27.1.1.6 (in ASME interpretations for 2007b-2010) already requires automatic verification of the 'telephone line or equivalent means', with failure flagged within 24 h. Altering emergency signaling devices is 'minor work' in NJ. UNVERIFIED: whether a copper-to-cellular swap triggers A17.1-2022 2.27.1, and who the AHJ is for Rutgers.

**Implication for Last Copper.** Weakens the hook, narrows the moat, does not kill. (1) Fix the deadline claim. NJ IBC 3001.2 is not retroactive. It does not become mandatory for Rutgers' existing elevators in Feb 2027. Feb 17, 2027 is only when new permit applications lose the option to use the old code. It binds new and completely replaced elevators, and possibly emergency-signaling alterations if A17.1 8.7.2.28 pulls in the full 2.27.1 (unverified). Saying 'mandatory mid-Feb 2027' to a faculty judge who knows codes would cost credibility. Reframe: 'every new NJ elevator from Feb 2027 must have visual and audible communication; the existing fleet, including Rutgers', has no such duty, and Deaf text mode closes that gap voluntarily.' (2) The 'nightly test' is partly code already. A17.1 2.27.1.1.6 has required automatic verification of the phone line with a 24-hour maximum since roughly 2007b/2010. Certified cellular communicators sell line supervision. PROVE must lead with what code does NOT check: end-to-end in-car audio (mic and speaker), the delivered location label versus 2.27.1.1.3(f), and answer and redirect time (45 s, 2.27.1.1.2). Pre-2010 cars have no supervision at all. (3) Feasibility cost. Putting a sensor mic or piezo in the car operating panel is licensed elevator-mechanic work (N.J.A.C. 13:44M) and likely 'minor work' with notice and a permit. Prototype on a bench or mock car, and pilot only with the Rutgers elevator contractor and AHJ. The zero-install dial-in option gains support from 2.27.1.1.4(a) (no intervention from inside the car, which reads as auto-answer). (4) Possible upside, unverified: if moving an elevator line from copper to a cellular gateway counts as an 'alteration to car emergency signaling devices' under 5:23-12.8(b)18, each NJ migration could pull in A17.1-2022 2.27.1 requirements, including text and video. That would make the copper sunset a code-forcing event and give PROVE's Deaf text mode real teeth. Ask the DCA Elevator Safety Unit or an elevator consultant this before the Feb 3 poster.

**Idea seeds:**
- Copper-migration code trap: confirm whether swapping an elevator phone line from Verizon copper to a cellular gateway counts as an 'alteration to car emergency signaling devices' (N.J.A.C. 5:23-12.8(b)18, A17.1 8.7.2.28). If yes, every NJ migration pulls in A17.1-2022 text and video, and Verizon's POTS-replacement channel needs a compliance kit.
- The code tolerates a day of silence: ASME Interpretation 13-343 allows up to 24 h between a dead line and the alarm, and line checks never test the in-car mic or speaker. Pitch: minutes, not a day, and audio, not just line.
- DCA ended routine six-month elevator inspections in 2017. Owners must instead keep maintenance checklists done by licensed mechanics. That is a documentation gap a tamper-evident digital checklist and test ledger could fill for AHJ annual inspections.
- Fragmented elevator jurisdiction: Piscataway = Local, New Brunswick = Third Party, Highland Park = State (DCA list, 4/1/2026). A cross-jurisdiction evidence format has a real audience.
- Rehab-subcode trigger: renovations covering more than 50% of a building's floor area force A17.3-2000 car emergency signaling upgrades on public elevators. The Rutgers capital-renovation pipeline is a predictable trigger list.
- NJ restored 3001.2 because 'availability for the equipment has greatly increased' (DCA, Oct 2025). A Verizon-network visual-communication retrofit for existing cars fits Pillar 3, though incumbents (AVIRE, Kings III) exist.
- A17.1-2022 2.27.1.1.2 requires a 45 s redirect if a call is not acknowledged. Measuring real answer and redirect times at Rutgers (HELP audit) gives primary data that maps directly to a code clause.

## 7. Verizon's 2026 copper retirement program (`copper_program`, confidence high)

VERIFIED from primary notices: Verizon's disclosures page lists 21 copper-retirement notices in 2026 across 20 states, and their stated counts sum to 1,566 wire centers. That is an undercount. Frontier's wholesale page adds a Minnesota notice (2026-03-A-MN, Aug 7, 163 wire centers) that Verizon's page omits, so the 2026 total is 1,729 stated wire centers in 21 states (1,432 Frontier, 297 legacy Verizon). My parse of the tables finds 1,763 unique CLLIs. 2025 had one notice (2 MA wire centers). The waves were Feb 9 (DE), Apr 15 (5 states), Aug 7 (8 states) and Sep 8 (8 states, including NJ/Piscataway, Dec 10). FCC, VERIFIED: four WCB waivers on Mar 20, 2025 (DA 25-248/250/251/252). FCC 25-37 NPRM (Jul 2025). FCC 26-19 Report and Order (Mar 26, 2026; effective May 20) made notices website-only, counted mobile wireless as an adequate replacement, made every discontinuance auto-grant on day 31, preempted states and refused an alarm-compatibility condition. DA 26-1038 (Sep 25, 2026) pulled the NJ-inclusive voice application (WC 26-227) from auto-grant. Verizon, VERIFIED: Skiadas (Jan and Apr 2026) put copper decommissioning, recycling and monetizing in the $5B cost program. Schulman (Jul 2026) said copper-emptied central offices are becoming edge data centers. The only dollar figure is Malady's 2022 ~$180M/yr. UNVERIFIED: POTS price increases, a 2026 savings figure, and the scope of WC 26-252 (secondary source only). The web-search budget was exhausted, so this pass ran zero searches and used direct primary fetches only.

**Implication for Last Copper.** Net effect: Last Copper gets stronger on the problem and riskier on the date. (1) Correct the number. '1,566 in 20 states' is Verizon's page only. The real 2026 count is 1,729 stated wire centers in 21 states (22 notices), plus a Sep 9 application for 9 more states and DC (secondary source). Say '1,700+ wire centers in 21 states this year'. The scale is bigger than claimed. (2) The regulatory void is now on the record from the FCC itself, in FCC 26-19 (effective May 20, 2026). Notices are website-only, mobile wireless counts as an adequate replacement, everything auto-grants on day 31, alarm compatibility 'sunset in 2025', AICC's G.711 condition was refused, and AICC counts 5 million dialer alarm panels. The FCC only 'encourage[s] carriers to ... prevent alarm signaling failures'. PROVE is the evidence that Verizon did what the FCC encouraged. That line is the strongest Content and Impact hook in the pitch. (3) Date risk. The FCC pulled the NJ-inclusive voice application (26-227) from auto-grant on Sep 25, 2026. Verizon says it discontinues services before retiring copper, so the Dec 10 Piscataway countdown may slip. Do not build the video around 'Dec 10'. Frame it as 'Verizon's 2026 program, 1,700+ wire centers, Piscataway in the Dec 10 wave, now under FCC review'. Business B1 and PBX-trunk lines (26-228) may already be authorized from Oct 23 (unconfirmed), and those are the lines elevator and alarm phones use, so the urgency may actually be earlier. (4) Verizon money. Executives frame copper as cost-out: decommission, recycle, monetize copper, and turn central offices into edge data centers. They gave no 2026 savings figure, so keep labeled assumptions and never credit copper savings to PROVE. 615 hostile filings show cutover risk is real and visible, which supports the 'lower cutover risk / Cutover Watch' value claim. (5) Crowding risk is rising, because copper retirement is now national news. Our edge has to stay primary data plus the FCC-26-19 alarm gap.

**Idea seeds:**
- FCC 26-19 asks carriers to configure IP networks 'to prevent alarm signaling failures' but imposes no test. A network-side 'Alarm Signal Assurance' harness would place synthetic Contact ID and SIA DTMF calls over Verizon VoLTE and POTS-replacement paths, catching RFC 2833/G.711 transcoding breaks per wire center before cutover. It is Verizon-native (Content), can graft into PROVE, and directly answers the AICC record (5M dialer panels).
- 'Copper out, compute in': Schulman says copper-emptied central offices are becoming power-ready edge data centers, and a trial sold out in 24 hours. The Piscataway CO (4 Skiles Ave, PSWYNJPI) sits beside Busch campus and is in the Dec 10 wave. Pitch it as Rutgers' anchor tenant for a CO-to-edge-inference conversion (Rutgers AI and robotics labs as demand). This is a unique local, Verizon-program-specific idea.
- Objection-to-resolution engine: 26-227 drew 615 filings, and the FCC pulled auto-grant. Public ECFS comments (no CPNI) could be classified by issue type and wire center and paired with student drive and indoor coverage tests that produce per-objection evidence for Verizon to resolve faster. Verizon's pain is a delayed multi-state discontinuance.
- Copper monetization and lead sheath: Skiadas says Verizon is 'recycling ... and monetizing that copper', and the 10-K flags lead-sheathed cable risk. A sustainability-pillar tool could estimate recoverable copper and lead-sheath tonnage per retiring wire center (1,700+ CLLIs from the public notices) and track chain of custody.
- Timeline arbitrage: business B1, PBX-trunk and Centrex discontinuance (26-171, 26-228) may take effect earlier than the voice and copper dates (Oct 23 and Nov 16, 2026). Institutions' elevator and alarm lines sit on exactly these business services, which is a distinct 'business-line-first' risk map.
- The public 2026 notice dataset, 1,763 wire-center CLLIs with addresses and dates (CSV built), can be joined to public building, elevator and hospital datasets to estimate life-safety exposure per wire center nationally without any CPNI.

## 8. Evidence that life-safety devices fail in network sunsets (`migration_incidents`, confidence medium)

Verified, from primary sources: network sunsets leave life-safety devices stranded at very large scale, and they fail silently. (1) AICC's May 10, 2021 FCC petition counted 'nearly six million' 3G alarm radios, about 40% of them on Verizon ('2.6 million homes and businesses'). It said each swap is a truck roll of up to 3 hours, that about 25% of appointments get no access, and that 'Lives will very likely be lost'. AT&T shut down 3G anyway in Feb 2022. (2) ADT still had about 250,000 sites on 3G/CDMA at YE2021 after spending $292M. It admits some customers who did not respond suffered 'loss of signal'. (3) After Sandy, Verizon told the FCC that its Voice Link copper substitute on Fire Island and the NJ barrier islands did not support 'some medical alert devices, and some (but not all) other monitoring systems like alarm systems' (FCC 14-185). (4) FCC 26-19 (Mar 2026) quotes AICC: 'five million installed [alarm] panels' still use dialer protocols. The FCC refused an alarm-compatibility condition and only 'encourage[s]' carriers to prevent alarm signaling failures. (5) At UAA, copper faults left blue-light phones intermittently dead for about 3 years, and only annual night walks found it; its elevator phones and fire systems were on the same copper. NOT found (UNVERIFIED): any dated US report of an elevator phone, fire-alarm communicator or call box going dead because of copper retirement or POTS discontinuance, and UK/Australian telecare and lift-phone cases. All WebSearch calls were refused because the shared search budget was exhausted. I used direct fetches of FCC, SEC and cited primary URLs instead, not the 8 searches the task asked for.

**Implication for Last Copper.** On balance this strengthens the PROBLEM slide but does not prove PROVE's specific edge, and I would not lead with it. Strengthens: (1) Primary-source numbers now back the claim that migrations strand life-safety devices: about 6M 3G alarm radios (40% on Verizon), about 5M dialer panels still in service in 2026, ADT stranding non-responders even after $292M, and a 25% no-access rate on truck rolls. That makes FIND (locating the lines) and the 'move first, never just cut' worklist credible. (2) The FCC has explicitly refused an alarm-compatibility mandate and only 'encourages' carriers to avoid alarm signaling failures. Nobody is required to prove these devices still work after a cutover, which is exactly the gap a Cutover Watch / PROVE record fills. (3) The FCC pausing Verizon's 2026 application makes 'evidence that lifelines survive the cutover' commercially useful to Verizon. Weakens or cautions: (a) I found NO documented case of an elevator phone or fire-alarm communicator going dead because of a copper retirement. The documented harm is residential alarms and PERS on 3G, plus aging-copper faults (UAA). A Verizon network judge can fairly say 'show me the elevator incident.' Do not claim one. (b) Every documented failure is a transport-level loss of signal, which existing line supervision catches: A17.1 2.27.1.1.6 within 24 h, NFPA 72 at 60 min or 6 h. None of this evidence shows dead speakers or mics, or wrong location labels, after migration. PROVE's acoustic and location edge still rests on reasoning, not incidents. (c) The 2013 Voice Link episode is Verizon's own NJ precedent and very on-point, but citing it in front of Verizon judges reads as a critique. Use it at most as 'lessons the industry learned', never as a gotcha. (d) MOVE is a mature, crowded vendor business (ADT, Alarm.com dealers, NAPCO, which says Rutgers already bought its StarLink), so keep MOVE as partner hardware. Net: keep Last Copper. Use AICC/ADT/FCC numbers for scale and the FCC 26-19 'encourage only' line for the regulatory gap, and treat 'no incident yet, because nobody checks' as a hypothesis the Rutgers HELP audit must test.

**Idea seeds:**
- Alarm-signal integrity on Verizon's replacement path: FCC 26-19 refused to require G.711 with RFC 2833 disabled for calls to alarm receivers, yet about 5M dialer panels remain. A Verizon-side canary could send Contact ID / SIA DTMF test bursts over One Talk, VoLTE and BPC100 paths every night and certify each wire center 'alarm-safe' before cutover. It is network-native, uses no CPNI, and fits Cutover Watch.
- Transition-clock gap as a metric: analog sunset gave 5 yrs for about 1M radios, 2G gave 4 yrs for about 2.4M, 3G gave 3 yrs for about 6M, while copper retirement needs only 90 days' notice (51.333) plus a 31-day auto-grant. A 'devices per day of runway' index per wire center could prioritize migrations.
- No-access truck rolls: AICC says about 25% of migration appointments end without access, and ADT stranded customers who never responded. A remote pre-staging and scheduling agent (ship a gateway, verify by test call, book the tech only when needed) attacks Verizon's truck-roll cost.
- Verizon migrated about 2.6M 3G alarm homes and businesses by Dec 31, 2022. That institutional data could train FIND's lifeline classifier, and it means Verizon has run a device-sunset playbook before.
- Silent-failure evidence: 'Even if your alarm appears to function, it will no longer communicate' (alarm dealer, 2022). A consumer-side 'does my alarm/medical pendant still reach the center?' self-test via the Verizon app is a Pillar 3 angle, but it is residential and off-campus.
- UAA's elevator phones, fire systems and blue lights all rode the same deteriorating copper, found only by annual night walks. A campus-wide 'one copper pair, many lifelines' dependency map may matter more than per-device testing.

## 9. Verizon's POTS replacement offer and ThingSpace (`verizon_pots_offer`, confidence medium)

VERIFIED (Verizon primary pages, fetched 2026-10-21). Verizon's POTS Replacement offer has four blocks:
(1) Business Phone Connect: Inseego BPC100 ($129.99 retail), ATEL V810V and V810VD. These are SMB VoLTE adapters, Contact-ID alarms only, with AA battery backup (BPC100: 23 h standby, 3 h talk).
(2) One Talk, 'starts at $20 per line'.
(3) 4G LTE / 5G Business Internet.
(4) Life-and-safety lines (elevator, fire alarm), handed to partners CSG (POTSolve, 'exclusive to Verizon', 12-24 h battery) and MarketSpark (CriticalConnect, 'monitored 24/7', dual-SIM across Verizon, AT&T and T-Mobile).

Verizon itself advertises no line test or end-to-end verification, only 'programmable notifications', failover and battery. Partners do monitoring: MarketSpark's Command Center, Ooma's RDM power/battery alerts, and Kings III 'auto testing' (unverified). None found advertises an acoustic speaker/mic test.

Ooma AirDial lists 'Verizon Certified'.

ThingSpace self-serve 'Jump in' is verified: 1-100 devices, prepaid by credit card, $1.50/mo (1 MB) to $60/mo (10 GB). It is data, SMS and MMS only with no voice, and includes Diagnostics.

The Device Diagnostics and Connectivity Management APIs exist (connection history, reachability, callbacks). RF data needs LwM2M-certified modules plus a Bundled Services Subscription with no published price.

UNVERIFIED: real POTSolve or MarketSpark per-line prices, LTE Business Internet and Business Digital Voice 2026 prices, Granite EPIK or Telguard ties to Verizon, and the A17.1 daily self-check requirement (vendor blog only).

The web-search budget ran out after about 19 searches, so later checks used direct fetches only.

**Implication for Last Copper.** This reshapes the champion without killing it.

The core gap is confirmed. Verizon's own POTS offer has no line-test or end-to-end verification feature, and it pushes elevator and fire-alarm lines to multi-carrier partners. MarketSpark ships dual SIMs including AT&T and T-Mobile, and AirDial is certified on all three carriers. So 'retention at a forced migration' is a real Verizon problem even inside its own channel.

The self-serve ThingSpace line for the PROVE supervisor is verified and cheap: $1.50-$6/mo, credit card, minutes to activate, Diagnostics included.

The MOVE detail must change. The dossier says elevators move to 'Inseego BPC100, ATEL V810V ... with 24-hour battery backup per Verizon's spec'. That is wrong for elevators. The BPC100 is an SMB adapter running on 3 AA batteries (23 h standby, 3 h talk), supports Contact-ID only, and Verizon never markets it for elevators. Verizon's own life-safety path is CSG POTSolve or MarketSpark. A Verizon judge would catch this.

The gateway voice line cannot ride a self-serve ThingSpace plan, which is data, SMS and MMS only. It needs a business voice line or a partner service.

The moat is narrower. Partners already sell '24/7 monitoring', portals and power/battery alerts. Kings III claims auto-testing, and a vendor says A17.1 already requires a daily path self-check without a test call (unverified). PROVE must not be pitched as 'monitoring'. It must be pitched as the only end-to-end proof that the speaker, mic, location label and answering point work, plus Cutover Watch across a wire center, sold as a partner-neutral layer on Verizon's ThingSpace APIs.

Action: rewrite MOVE as 'Verizon life-safety partners (CSG/MarketSpark) or a Verizon-certified gateway'. Delete the '24-hour per Verizon spec' line. Add one slide titled 'what partners monitor vs what PROVE proves'.

**Idea seeds:**
- Verizon's own life-safety channel is multi-carrier (MarketSpark dual SIM on Verizon, AT&T and T-Mobile; AirDial certified on all three). A 'Verizon-first proof' layer that makes the Verizon path the verified primary is a concrete retention lever.
- BPC100 footnote: only Contact-ID alarm protocol is supported, and other central-station protocols are 'not supported'. A pre-cutover alarm-protocol audit (which panels use SIA, 4/2 or modem formats) would catch silent failures before copper is cut.
- A17.1 2.27.1.1.5 requires 4 h of alternate power for the visual indicator and two-way voice, but the BPC100 runs on 3 AA batteries (3 h talk). Battery-truth telemetry and a power-outage drill for migrated life-safety lines is a measurable gap.
- The ThingSpace Connectivity Management API exposes connection history, reachability status and callbacks. A wire-center 'Cutover Watch' could be prototyped on real ThingSpace data for $1.50-$6 per line per month with no Verizon partnership.
- POTSolve's flyer sells 'advanced diagnostics' as the reason to leave copper, yet no test feature is specified. Verizon's own marketing promise is unbacked, which is a direct Content-score hook.
- MarketSpark's FDNY MFVN acceptance under 2016 NFPA 72 means cellular fire-alarm paths are now AHJ-acceptable in NYC. Check whether NJ AHJs and Rutgers' fire marshal follow suit, as a primary-data question only Rutgers students can ask.

## 10. Competition intelligence: winners, judges, what Verizon wants (`competition_intel`, confidence medium)

VERIFIED (primary pages, read 2026-10-21): Rutgers 2025-26 winners were AeroBin, EchoClass/SafeRoute and RU Experience. Seven finalists each gave a 10-minute talk followed by judges' questions (SoE, Apr 13 2026). The 2024-25 winners were UNIVIEW, AccessED and RUNetworking. The 2024-25 Verizon judges came from network strategy and planning: SVP Strategy & Transformation Mike Haberman, an AVP of Network Planning and a Senior Director of Network Planning. Rutgers' Dean, CIO and Vice Provost also judged. The program runs out of Verizon Global Networks & Technology's Technology Ambassador Office, which Haberman leads. Its coordinator, Suzanne Schnaars, wants 'project work that also advances Verizon's initiatives' (UTD, Jun 2026). The 2026-27 brief changed from the 2025 'enhance a personal experience' framing to 'clear, measurable business value' tied to one of three pillars. Winners elsewhere included non-consumer work: AeroBin's $100/unit operations retrofit, NOTED (subscription B2B), Vanguard (premium network routing) and the Northeastern crash system built on Verizon Smart Intersections. The briefing's line 'past winners were all consumer apps' is therefore wrong. The 2026-27 programme also runs at NJIT, UTD and probably Northeastern. NJIT: video Nov 12 2026, Winner's Circle Mar 30 2027 at Basking Ridge. UNVERIFIED: the Northeastern pages (TLS errors, 503s), the 2026-27 Rutgers judges, team counts at Rutgers, what 'Assign Business Teams' means, and LinkedIn posts (snippets only). The search budget ran out after about 19 searches, so the analog-challenge evidence is thin.

**Implication for Last Copper.** On balance this strengthens Last Copper, but it raises the bar on accuracy. Strengthening: (1) Verizon runs the contest from Global Networks & Technology. The Technology Ambassador Office sits under the SVP of Strategy & Transformation, whose remit is moving the network to its 'architecture of the future'. The Verizon judges we can name are network-planning and network-engineering leaders. Copper retirement is a live GN&T transformation program with an FCC filing, which is exactly the 'problem from industry' that 'advances Verizon's initiatives' the coordinators say they want. (2) The 2026-27 brief rewards 'clear, measurable business value', and AeroBin shows that an operations retrofit with a unit price can win, so the 'consumer apps only' premise in the briefing is false. (3) Verizon has a separate retention project at NJIT, which fits LC's retention framing. (4) In 2025 the Rutgers CIO (whose OIT owns the phone lines) sat on the final panel. If she returns, Gate A data from OIT gives us a credible insider story. Risks: (a) Network-planning judges know copper retirement and POTS replacement intimately. Any overclaim gets caught: that nobody finds life-safety lines, that FIND needs CPNI, or that Verizon lacks line-type codes. Pitch LC as making Verizon's migration safer and provable, never as exposing a gap or criticizing Verizon. (b) The rubric's Ubiquity (/10) and the 'campus experience' language still favor many-user problems. Quantify riders per elevator per day and lines per wire center nationally (1,566 wire centers). (c) AeroBin won partly on a working, priced, externally validated prototype. PROVE needs a bench demo of the two-direction acoustic test and a BOM under $150 before Feb 3. (d) The 2-minute video duration is certified, so a hard cap at 2:00 is mandatory. Verdict: keep LC as champion. Lead with a named Verizon initiative and a priced per-line pilot. Recruit a business or finance teammate before the mid-December team assignment.

**Idea seeds:**
- The judges come from Verizon network planning and engineering (GN&T), not Consumer. Any pitch that solves a GN&T operations problem (cutover risk, truck rolls, site energy, provisioning errors) speaks the judges' language better than a student app.
- The 2026-27 brief moved from 'enhance a personal experience' to 'clear, measurable business value' plus three pillars. Most LLM-assisted teams are still writing to the old framing, which is left over in the FAQ and the 2025 PDF.
- Winning SoE projects become RBS go-to-market case material, and the 2026-27 timeline says 'Assign Business Teams' in mid-December. A pitch built with a GTM-ready business case (unit price, buyer, channel) can travel further.
- The guidelines say winners may be invited to a 'technical trial'. Propose a trial-ready 30-day pilot with Verizon (named lines, named metrics) as the ask in the video and the poster.
- AeroBin's playbook: WINLAB internship, then the Columbia CS3 accelerator, then Rutgers Shark Tank 2nd place, then the Smart Campus win. External validation before the finals plus a stated unit price ($100) and savings claim. Copy the structure: a priced BOM plus one outside endorsement before Feb 3.
- UTD's Vanguard won with a premium network-routing product for first responders. Network-native features (priority, routing, assurance) are rewarded when tied to a paying customer.
- NJIT's HOP surveyed students and parents at both NJIT and Rutgers, and UTD's NOTED interviewed physicians. Primary interviews with the people who answer Rutgers HELP calls (RUPD/RUSSECC) would score under 'Researched' and are something only Rutgers students can collect.
- The NJIT, Rutgers and probably Northeastern winners meet at a joint Winner's Circle, likely Mar 30 2027 at Basking Ridge (inferred from NJIT's page). A pitch that scales across all four host campuses ('every campus elevator on retiring copper') fits that room.

## 11. Visual / text elevator communication for Deaf riders (`deaf_elevator`, confidence medium)

Verified: a Deaf text mode is no longer a differentiator. It is code-required on new cars and sold by OEMs and independents. ASME A17.1-2019 added in-car two-way text messages (2.27.1.1.3(c)-(e), including responses from riders who cannot speak or hear) and video of the car floor (k). Both remain in A17.1-2022. IBC 3001.2 has changed every edition: 2018 required video-based text; 2021 required 'visible text and audible modes'; 2024 requires 'visual and audible' per A17.1. NJ deleted 3001.2 until R.2026 d.086 (effective Aug 17, 2026). DCA said equipment availability had 'greatly increased'. The section is not retroactive. Federal ADA 2010 407.4.9 requires no text, only operable parts and tactile labels, plus A17.1's visual acknowledgement light. Shipping products: RATH SmartView 2 (AVIRE/Halma; retrofit units), MAD MosaicONE VMS (phone-agnostic retrofit; YES/NO via door buttons; line monitoring), Schindler Visual Communication System (YES/NO via door buttons; cites IBC-2018 3001.2/A17.1-2019), and Otis ONE Pro ('video and text emergency communications'). Schindler SafeCall and JANUS already sell wireless migration plus line tests or 'end-to-end' verification. Unverified: KONE/TKE products, Kings III displays, any lawsuits or complaints (WebSearch budget was exhausted, so only direct primary-page fetches were used), and fleet adoption rates. The remaining gap is the existing NJ fleet (non-retroactive), and retrofit products already exist for it.

**Implication for Last Copper.** Weakens Last Copper. It does not kill the concept, but it kills Deaf text mode as a selling point. The storyboard beat 'Help is coming. Are you hurt? YES/NO' is close to Schindler's and MAD's shipping UI (YES/NO via door buttons). 'Last Copper does it today' would be called out by any elevator-literate judge. Remove Deaf text mode from the Creativity claim and from the three video beats. Reframe it in one of two ways. (1) Text mode is code-required for new cars, so PROVE verifies the visual path nightly alongside audio. (2) A voluntary, low-cost LTE-M text-only retrofit for the pre-2027 NJ fleet that NJ's 2018-2026 deletion left without it, stated honestly as below current A17.1 (no video) and never as compliance. Second blow: line-supervision incumbents are closer than assumed. MAD advertises line monitoring and is phone-agnostic, Schindler SafeCall advertises line tests and self-checks, and JANUS claims 'end-to-end' verification. PROVE's moat shrinks to the two-transducer in-car acoustic test, the location-label check and Cutover Watch, and each still needs proof that no incumbent does it. Upside to exploit: A17.1-2019 video and text means the elevator emergency link now needs DATA, which copper POTS cannot carry. That gives a clean Verizon content hook (every new or modernized NJ car after ~Feb 17, 2027 needs a broadband or cellular data path). Incumbents use multi-carrier SIMs, so Verizon's angle would have to be certification or bundling, not exclusivity.

**Idea seeds:**
- Code-forced data: A17.1-2019 2.27.1.1.3(k) video plus (c)-(e) text mean an elevator emergency link needs data, not just voice. POTS cannot carry it. Every new NJ elevator permitted under IBC/2024 (after the ~Feb 17, 2027 grace period) needs a broadband or cellular data path. A Verizon 'elevator data line' (Cat-1/RedCap) certified with RATH, MAD and Schindler is a cleaner Content story than a voice-only POTS swap.
- Orphaned first-generation visual systems: RATH SmartView 1 stopped being recommended Dec 1 2024, took final orders Feb 1 2025, and needed a SmartView Hub account from Jan 27 2025 or it cannot be monitored or tested. That is a sunset that mirrors the copper sunset, with a measurable count of affected cars.
- Primary data only Rutgers can collect: a walk audit of Busch and Livingston car stations (LED-only vs display vs camera) set against the 2027 new-build standard. That quantifies the 'existing-fleet accessibility gap' that NJ's 2018-2026 deletion of 3001.2 created.
- First usability study of code-mandated elevator text messages with Deaf and hard-of-hearing Rutgers students (via Disability Services): comprehension of preset messages, the YES/NO door-button convention, and time to respond. The code mandates the messages but not their usability, and no study was found.
- Answering-point readiness: A17.1 requires authorized personnel to send text and view video. Does the Rutgers answering point (RUPD/RUSSECC or the contractor's call center) have any console that can answer a text or video elevator call? This is a gap in who answers, not in car hardware.
- Code patchwork map: MN, CT, OR and UT deleted 3001.2, FL omits it, IN and WI predate it, and 2018/2021/2024 texts differ (video, then text, then A17.1). Multi-state owners and university systems face inconsistent duties, which could become a compliance-intelligence product.
- Vendor citation errors: AVIRE's May 2026 AHJ guide cites clause numbers that do not match the A17.1 text and an ADA section (407.2.2) about hall signals. Inspectors relying on vendor guides may mis-inspect, which suggests a neutral, clause-accurate inspection checklist or evidence record.

## 12. LTE-M / Cat-1 indoors, dual path, network sunsets (`ltem_indoor`, confidence medium)

VERIFIED (primary sources fetched 2026-10-21): (1) Indoor coverage is a known, admitted problem. UNC-Chapel Hill lists 'cell signal strength and reliability' as the limitation of its cellular elevator phones. NC's state POTS guidance lists 'additional antenna and/or signal boosters'. Verizon partner MarketSpark says POTS-over-LTE is not plug-and-play, adds antennas, and documents 'full bars' sites that still failed. NAPCO fire communicators pick the strongest of Verizon, AT&T or T-Mobile at each site and sell 100-ft external antennas. (2) Nobody checks indoor coverage. FCC 26-19 (Mar 2026) accepts National Broadband Map mobile coverage as an 'adequate replacement' and declined extra verification, and 47 CFR 1.7004(c)(5) requires those maps to show outdoor coverage only. (3) Dual path: NFPA 72 allows a single supervised path. Incumbents sell dual-carrier or cell+IP. We found no elevator dual-path rule. (4) No Verizon LTE or LTE-M sunset was found. Verizon touts a '15-year runway' and 'no imminent plans' to turn down NB-IoT. T-Mobile is the carrier refarming LTE (about 2028, reported). (5) RedCap on Verizon in 2026 is live but early: a product page, LTE fallback, 'start Fast Track certification now', Apple Watch 5G support, and SA nationwide with gaps. UNVERIFIED: any measured Verizon LTE-M or Cat-1 signal in elevator machine rooms or basements; vendor dBm thresholds; whether Verizon enables LTE-M CE Mode B or VoLTE over Cat-M1; whether Rutgers fire communicators (NAPCO, 2020) are already off copper. Method: per the briefing, no WebSearch; about 25 Google News RSS searches plus direct fetches.

**Implication for Last Copper.** Net: this STRENGTHENS PROVE and WEAKENS MOVE as written. Strengthens: the federal test for 'adequate' wireless replacement is an outdoor map that the FCC refused to verify further, while vendors admit some buildings have 'terrible cell service' and 'full bars still fail'. Nobody independently proves that the line in the machine room works, so a nightly end-to-end test plus RF telemetry is a real gap. The 2026 Verizon outages (January software outage, August fiber vandalism and SOS) make Cutover Watch concrete. Verizon's '15-year runway' versus T-Mobile's LTE refarm is a usable Verizon-specific argument for putting lifelines on Verizon. Weakens: (a) the incumbents are multi-carrier and pick the strongest network per site (MarketSpark dual SIM, NAPCO Verizon/AT&T/T-Mobile auto-select, AirDial LTE+Ethernet MultiPath), so 'move every line to Verizon LTE' will lose in some basements and sounds naive to network-planning judges. Reframe MOVE as 'Verizon primary, measured; second path where the survey says so'. (b) Voice gateways (for example the BPC100) are Cat-4 or Cat-1, so LTE-M's deep-indoor benefit does not carry the voice call, and deep coverage (CE Mode B) trades away voice. Stop implying that 'LTE-M' fixes elevator voice; keep LTE-M only for the PROVE supervisor. (c) Rutgers fire-alarm communicators may have been on NAPCO dual-path cellular since 2020, which shrinks FIND/MOVE to elevators, refuge areas and blue lights. (d) RedCap video stays roadmap only. Action: the escorted machine-room RF survey (RSRP, RSRQ, SINR, attach) is now the single most valuable primary data. A line like 'the FCC map says covered; X of Y Rutgers machine rooms say otherwise' is unique and fresh.

Signal Boost™ Circuitry & unique dual-diversity twin antennas, maximizing signal acquisition and eliminating the multiphase-effect signal-clash/drop-outs single-antenna units are prone to."* | brochure PDF created 202 | https://www.napcosecurity.com/media/pdfs/A893-StarLink-MaxFire3.pdf |

| verified | Dual-path rules, summarised. Fire alarms (NFPA 72, per sibling research): a single cellular path is allowed if supervised at least every 60 minutes; with multiple paths, each is supervised within 6 hours. Incumbent fire communicators already offer cell+IP or dual-carrier cell-only dual path. Elevators: no source found requiring dual path. A17.1 requires automatic line verification with failure flagged within 24 h (per founder update). The NC guidance recommends one dedicated line per car. <br>*"Multiple communications paths shall be arranged so that a single point of failure shall not cause more than a single path to fail."* | NFPA 72-2019 text as quo | https://www.redmond.gov/DocumentCenter/View/32016 |

**Idea seeds:**
- 'The Map Is Outdoors': FCC 26-19 (Mar 2026) accepts outdoor-only mobile maps (47 CFR 1.7004(c)(5)) as an adequate copper replacement and refuses extra verification. Rutgers students measure indoor RSRP, RSRQ and attach at every lifeline location (machine rooms, basements, refuge areas) and give Verizon a per-location 'indoor coverage attestation' to answer objections like those that paused its NJ filing (DA 26-1038) and drew NE/MN complaints.
- Lifeline longevity as a Verizon differentiator: T-Mobile restricts new LTE-only business activations (Jan 2026) and plans to refarm LTE by about 2028 (reported), while Verizon promises a '15-year runway' and 'no imminent plans' for NB-IoT. Pitch: life-safety devices belong on the network that commits not to strand them. Multi-carrier boxes that auto-pick T-Mobile today may be stranded.
- Carrier choice is a one-time snapshot: NAPCO/MarketSpark units pick the best carrier at power-up, but indoor RF changes (refarm, new glass, outages). Re-evaluate nightly and switch with Verizon's own IoT Network Continuity eSIM (flyer dated Oct 2026), so Verizon wins back lines it lost at install.
- 'Full bars still fail' (MarketSpark): RSRQ and SINR, not bars, decide whether alarm or voice transmissions succeed. A low-cost RSRQ/SINR logger in machine rooms is a sellable install-acceptance test.
- Verizon's 2026 outage record (Jan 16 software outage affecting about 2M; Aug 9 SoCal fiber vandalism with phones in SOS; NJ SOS headlines Aug 18) as evidence for a carrier-side 'lifeline impact' view: which life-safety lines in an outage footprint went dark.
- RedCap on Verizon is live but early (Fast Track certification, Apple Watch 5G, LTE fallback). A RedCap elevator-car video and text endpoint for A17.1-2019 is a plausible 2027 Verizon showcase, but only as a roadmap.

## 13. Post-June-2026 Verizon facts (`sweep_verizon`, confidence medium)

Most facts here come from primary sources (Verizon newsroom, FCC PDFs, ECFS API metadata). Q3 2026 results come out Oct 26, so there are no Q3 headlines yet; consensus is about $34.9B revenue and $1.28 EPS. Q2 2026 (Jul 24): 184K postpaid phone adds, record $13.7B adjusted EBITDA, revenue down 0.7%, guidance raised, $397M severance, and the international business held for sale ahead of the BT JV. AI Connect: a Google dark-fiber deal worth more than $1B, central offices on retired copper reportedly being retrofitted as edge data centers, and the Corning fiber deal (Sep 8). Customer experience: Simplicity, Verizon One and Loyalty (Jun 16), plus free safety and AI perks (Oct 14). Cost: 274 stores sold and about 500 corporate jobs cut (Jul 16). Network: satellite JV with AT&T and T-Mobile (Oct 1), 6GIF and ISAC crowd sensing (Sep 14), a public-safety slicing blog, and the Frontline survey (Oct 13, NJ). Rutgers: Visible sponsors Rutgers football (Sep 22). Key for the champion: on Sep 25 the FCC pulled Verizon's 9-state legacy voice discontinuance (WC 26-227, including NJ) from automatic grant. The business-line application (WC 26-228) was not named. Not confirmed: whether 26-228 was granted, whether Piscataway is covered (ECFS PDFs returned 403), satellite launch status, and outages after August. The search budget ran out after about 17 searches. One SEC request mistakenly sent the user's email in its User-Agent; this was not repeated.

**Implication for Last Copper.** This news strengthens Last Copper's case to Verizon but weakens its countdown. (1) The Dec 10 date is now soft. Verizon's NJ copper notice says it will discontinue services under FCC rules before retiring the copper. On Sep 25 the FCC pulled the residential and business POTS discontinuance (26-227, incl. Verizon New Jersey) from automatic grant, with no new date. A video built on 'cut on Dec 10' could look wrong by Feb 3. (2) The business-line application (26-228: B1 lines, PBX trunks, key systems, ISDN), which is where university elevator and alarm lines usually sit, was not named in the pause. If it auto-granted on Sep 26, those lines could be discontinued on or after Oct 23, 2026, which makes the problem more urgent for institutions. This must be checked in ECFS before relying on it, along with whether Piscataway is in the NJ service area. (3) The fight at the FCC is now about whether replacement service is 'comparable' and safe: 911 concerns, a flood of comments, county ex partes, and Verizon's Oct 9 supplemental letter. Nightly records proving that migrated life-safety lines work are exactly the evidence Verizon needs to unblock its own discontinuances. Recommend reframing from 'countdown to Dec 10' to 'the evidence that gets the paused retirements approved'. (4) A new value link: retired-copper central offices become AI Connect edge data centers (Light Reading). Every stalled wire center delays that conversion, so Cutover Watch has a CFO story beyond retention. (5) Risk: Verizon may slow-walk retirements where customers object, which would reduce urgency at Rutgers.

**Idea seeds:**
- Docket 26-227 is a live public dataset (ECFS API: hundreds of comments Sep-Oct 2026, county ex partes, a Verizon supplemental letter). An LLM classifier of objection types (911, medical alert, alarm, elevator, rural coverage), matched to Verizon's remedy for each, would be fresh public data no other team's LLM knows about.
- Copper-retired central offices are becoming AI Connect edge data centers (Light Reading, Jul 24). Verizon's New Brunswick C20 switch at 18 Paterson St is now absorbing South Amboy's traffic, which could anchor a 'what the New Brunswick CO becomes' angle.
- WC 26-228 targets B1 business lines, PBX trunks, key systems, ISDN BRI and payphones in NJ from Oct 23, 2026. A Rutgers PBX-trunk and B1 inventory via OPRA would be primary data that only Rutgers students can collect.
- The AT&T/T-Mobile/Verizon satellite JV (Oct 1) promises 'redundant connectivity when ground networks are unavailable'. Satellite as a third path for life-safety endpoints or campus emergency communications, as a roadmap item.
- The 6GIF ISAC trial did network-native crowd-density sensing on 40 MHz CBRS at a Dallas World Cup event. SHI Stadium as a candidate ISAC trial site (crowded space; would need differentiation).
- Verizon embeds Anthropic's Claude and runs autonomous agents, with 70M+ automated changes and fixes in under 2 minutes. A pitch built as an agent that plugs into Verizon's Level-4 autonomy program fits the CTO's own framing.
- Visible and Learfield sponsor Rutgers football (Sep 22): a Verizon Value marketing budget already sits on campus, with David Kim as the executive sponsor.
- Frontline survey: 77% of agencies rank resilience first, and daily AI use is about 32%. RUPD and RUSSECC are an instance of this, and the survey is NJ-datelined.
- Verizon Loyalty, Verizon Dollars and Shine is a new rewards platform for every customer. Campus-specific Shine drops or Verizon Dollars partners would be a customer-experience play (lower priority; consumer-app space is crowded).
- Verizon Business is #1 in JD Power 2026 for large enterprise internet. Verizon is spinning international wireline into the BT JV and refocusing on the domestic market, so US public-sector anchor customers like Rutgers matter more.

## 14. Post-June-2026 Rutgers facts (`sweep_rutgers`, confidence medium)

Verified from primary sources (budget PDFs, OIT alerts, IP&O pages, Daily Targum, Patch, rutgers.edu). Money: the FY27 $6.2B budget nets a 0.11% margin ($7.06M). There is a hiring freeze and a review of every vendor contract. IP&O Plant O&M is up 34% and NB Housing's surplus has collapsed to $93K. Ops owners: Henry Velez is now the permanent COO (IP&O includes the codes & standards office, public safety and emergency management), and a new Chief Risk Officer was appointed Oct 1. Infrastructure: the BOG approved hot-water piping replacement on Busch and Livingston (Oct 1). IT: a weekday network outage on Oct 12 ran about 6 hours, a power outage on Aug 7 took down Wi-Fi, wired network and VPN overnight, and a Windows update blocked the RBHS networks on Oct 14. The eduroam cutover means RUWireless Secure turns off Jan 5, 2027. Transit: Academy Bus has an on-time KPI above 85% (versus 77-78% historically), and RUSA and IP&O hold a town hall around Oct 22. SHI has a new egress plan, and Homecoming drew 48,650 with students climbing fences. Safety: theft dominates; there is backlash over Flock and AI cameras, plus off-campus fire and arson incidents. HELIX opened in July; its AI hub went to AWS and SHI hosts the $125M NJ supercomputer. Unverified or missing: Reddit (blocked), NJ.com, NJ Transit, Rutgers Health and fall-2026 housing data, and the Annual Security/Fire Report. The search budget ran out after 13 searches. No source covers Rutgers life-safety lines or copper.

**Implication for Last Copper.** Moderately strengthens the champion, with one cut. (1) Strongest new argument: the campus IP network is not a safe lifeline path. Oct 12 was about 6 hours down on a weekday with no root cause given, Aug 7 lost power to Wi-Fi, wired network and VPN for about 15 hours, and Apr 27 a data center lost power. A lifeline OIT moved to campus VoIP would have gone silent during each outage. Drop or demote the 'Rutgers wired network as optional second path' and pitch Verizon LTE plus a gateway battery as the independent path. (2) Austerity fits the pitch: 0.11% margin, a hiring freeze and a vendor-contract review favor a cost-neutral line swap with automated nightly proof that needs no new staff. A pitch that adds net spend would fail. (3) Clear buyers now exist: COO Velez (codes & standards, public safety, emergency management) and new CRO Stallmann (enterprise risk). (4) Poster hook: two forced cutovers 26 days apart, Verizon copper on or after Dec 10 and RUWireless Secure off Jan 5, 2027. PROVE must not ride campus Wi-Fi. (5) Cut: the Flock/AI-camera backlash makes the Phase-2 ambient-mic dial-in politically toxic. Shelve it. Nothing found here resolves Gate A (which Rutgers lines ride Piscataway copper).

**Idea seeds:**
- Double cutover winter: an audit of which campus devices break when Verizon copper retires (Dec 10) and RUWireless Secure shuts off (Jan 5, 2027). Headless IoT and building devices that cannot re-enroll get Verizon LTE-M/ThingSpace fallback. Overlaps 'Callable Rooms'/'SIM Is the Key', so differentiate on non-phone devices.
- Lifeline path independence: measure how many building systems (elevator phones, fire panels, access control, BAS) route through campus network or power that failed on Oct 12, Aug 7 and Apr 27. Primary data: the OIT alert log plus a Facilities walk-down.
- IP&O cost-review angle: with every vendor contract under review, a 'telecom line rationalization' sweep (POTS, alarm lines, fax lines) that pays for itself. Data from OPRA invoices.
- Aging Piscataway utilities: the hot-water piping replacement on Busch and Livingston (approved Oct 1) suggests Verizon fiber/acoustic sensing of underground utility leaks. Overlaps 'Fiber That Listens', so check novelty.
- Academy bus KPI: the on-time target of 85-90% is contractual, and RUSA/IP&O hold a town hall around Oct 22. Students can collect independent stop-level arrival data. Crowded or explored ('Pass-Up Ledger', 'Contract Truth'), so low priority.
- SHI game-day egress: the new dedicated shuttle bus lane to Jersey Mike's Arena and the Homecoming gate overflow (48,650 attendance, fence-climbing). Crowd and stadium ideas are crowded, so avoid unless the hook is Verizon-specific.
- Off-campus rental fire/gas safety (88 Louis St fire, Hamilton St gas tampering, Delavan St fatality): LTE-M smoke/CO/gas sensing for landlord-owned student rentals. Overlaps 'FireLine NB'.
- HELIX and the AI ecosystem: AWS won the HELIX AI hub and SHI hosts the $125M supercomputer, so Verizon has no visible role in Rutgers' 2026 AI/health buildout. A Verizon-specific connectivity or edge offer for the new RWJMS tower could fill that gap. Data-center politics in NJ are hostile.

## 15. Post-June-2026 regulatory facts (`sweep_regulatory`, confidence high)

No WebSearch was used, per the briefing (search budget exhausted). Instead I queried primary sources: the Federal Register API (150 FCC documents, Jul 1 to Oct 21), FCC 26-34 to 26-69, 486 DA documents (26-620 to 26-1127), BPU agendas, NJ Legislature APIs and digests, and DCA pages.

VERIFIED:
(1) FCC 26-19 copper and discontinuance rules took effect Oct 15 2026. The FCC no longer issues copper-retirement notices and the objection process is gone. Direct notice now goes to 911 providers, but not to building owners.
(2) Verizon's separate WC 26-228 application (B1 business lines, PBX trunks and key systems, NJ included) carries an authorized date of Oct 23 2026. The FCC paused only WC 26-227. That 26-228 was granted is inferred, not confirmed.
(3) DA 26-1042/1043 (Sept 28): Verizon's NG911 rollout sent MLTS 911 calls to a national call center when location was missing. The FCC urges TDM PBX users to move to IP.
(4) FCC 26-63 NOI on the 911 framework: comments due Nov 16.
(5) Also verified: outage-notice rules, WEA FNPRM, robocall actions, TRS ASCII repeal, SpaceX D2D grant, and the Nov 17 EAS test.
(6) NJ: no telecom, 911 or elevator laws were signed and BPU agendas had no Verizon items. A5548 (outage credits) and S4782 (elevator keys) are pending.

UNVERIFIED: whether 26-228 was granted, NJ NG911 status, the 26-227 commenters, and any 988 or LBR action (none found).

**Implication for Last Copper.** NET: this STRENGTHENS the problem but changes the clock. Fix the pitch before Nov 8.

(a) Since Oct 15 the FCC's copper-retirement notice and objection machinery is gone. Notice is a post on Verizon's website plus direct notice to carriers and 911 providers. Nobody is required to tell the elevator owner or the alarm company. That is a clean, citable 'who finds the lifelines?' gap, dated after mid-2026.

(b) The 'paused' framing is only half true. WC 26-227 (legacy voice) is paused, but WC 26-228 covers exactly the line classes elevator and alarm lines ride on: B1 business lines, PBX trunks and key systems. Its listed area includes NJ, its date is on or after Oct 23 2026, and I found no removal notice. Verify in ECFS before saying it; if it was granted, the urgency comes back.

(c) DA 26-1042 is Verizon's own regulatory record of a silent transition failure: 911 calls sent to a national call center because location data was missing. That directly supports PROVE's location-assurance check and Cutover Watch, and it is a Verizon-specific operating program. Present it as Verizon fixing a problem, not as a gotcha.

(d) The 911 NOI comment date (Nov 16) gives a real external action: the team could file a short comment on life-safety endpoints in the IP transition.

WEAKENING:
- The WC 26-214 master docket gives customers a formal objection route.
- Neither BPU nor the Legislature is acting on copper.
- 'No one is notified' must be worded precisely, because 911 providers are notified.

Nothing here kills the champion.
